06Annual R eport 2025-2026INSULINS & GLP -1 –AN EV OLVING “MO AT” FOR Y OUR C OMP ANY
The e xciting mark et opportunit y in Insulins
The Indian insulins mark et has been dominated by innovators for a long time. With signiﬁcant compl exities in product technol ogy 
and manuf acturing, storage and transport as well as patient service/ engagement, this Rs. 5,000+ crore p.a. mark et is a natural 
oligopol y and has always been a strategic adjacency to our established Diabetes franchise. 
Accor dingl y, we enter ed this mark et in March 2022 with the launch of two homegr own brands Xsulin and Xglar. We subsequentl y 
acquir ed insulin brands Basal og and Insugen from Biocon in April 2024. With four brands being promoted by two of our 
divisions, our Insulins franchise has scaled up signiﬁcantl y and is evol ving into a sour ce of long- term competitive 
advant age for us.
Our Bhopal facilit y places us in a strategicall y competitive position to serve the Indian 
insulin mark et. With a manuf acturing capacit y of 130+ millions vials p.a. and 75+ 
million cartridges p.a., this facilit y will enabl e us to have adequate contr ol on suppl y and 
economics in the following product families:
On the back of our established front-end presence in Diabetes and our fully integrated Bhopal 
capabilities, we expect to be a strategicall y import ant player in the Indian insulin mark et, 
which is steadil y moving away from innovators and into the hands of Indian companies.• Existing produc ts – RHI and Glar gine

11 Annual R eport 2025-2026Dear Shar ehol ders
FY26 mark ed the achievement of yet another import ant 
milestone for your company – an operating revenue of 
Rs. 3,000+ crore – with a consolidated EBID TA mar gin 
of 36% and an EPS growth of 34% from continuing 
operations. This was a year of consolidation, 
acceleration and building long-term competitive 
advant age in line with the key themes I had highlighted 
to you last year . 
The ﬁrst highlight of the year w as the str engthening of 
our insulin franchise. Leveraging our leadership in anti-
diabetes together with our expanding capabilities in 
technol ogy, manuf acturing, and suppl y chain, we made 
meaningful progress in a categor y that is increasingl y 
shaping the futur e of diabetes care.
Our perf ormance was led by the strategicall y import ant 
RHI Cartridge segment, where our market share 
increased from 8% in April 2024 to 25% by the year -
end, representing a threefold expansion since the 
acquisition of the Biocon business. Across the broader 
RHI and Glar gine mark et, our shar e increased from 9% 
to 16%, putting us ﬁrmly on track to achieve our 
medium- term objective of 25%. These gains validate 
both our acquisition thesis and our abilit y to execute in a 
complex biologics business. On the back of this 
platf orm and a robust pipeline of several insulin anal og 
products (Degludec, Aspart and combinations), we are 
well positioned to enter the Rs. 2,000+ crore insulin 
anal ogs mark et as well. 
The second highlight of the year was our establishing 
an early lead in the GLP -1 therapy following the loss of 
exclusivity for Semaglutide in March 2026, with a 
mark et rank of #1 by sales volume and #2 by sales value 
in the inject able segment during the ﬁrst quarter post -
LOE. With in-house manuf acturing set to commence in 
Q2 FY27, we expect to strengthen our mark et position 
as well as operating mar gins.
Underpinning our Inject able Diabesit y journey is our 
Bhopal Biologics facility, an integrated biologics 
platform that provides sustainable competitive 
advant age across insulins, insulin anal ogs, and GLP -1 
therapies. Our objective is not merely to participate in 
these categories, but to build India's most integrated, 
competitive, and patient -centric diabetes platf orm.
Across our Domestic Branded Formulations business, 
the Dermatol ogy franchise continued to outperf orm the mark et, reinforcing its position as a key growth driver . 
While our Oral Anti-Diabetic (OHA) and Cardiac 
portf olios faced temporar y headwinds during the year , 
we remain conﬁdent of restoring growth momentum in 
FY27, supported by a strong pipeline of new product 
launches.
Consistent with our strategic focus on building high-
qualit y and differentiated businesses, we exited the 
Trade Generic s segment during the year , sharpening 
our portfolio and capital allocation priorities. Our 
Domestic Branded Formulations business deliver ed an 
industr y-leading EBITD A mar gin of 37% with a YPM of 
Rs. 5.7 lakh, underscoring the strength of our operating 
model and our continued leadership in productivit y and 
sust ained value creation.
The third highlight of the year was our decisive 
commitment to our next phase of growth by pulling 
forward strategic investments of ~ Rs. 400 crore, 
reinforcing our aspiration to continuousl y elevate our 
technology and manufacturing capabilities. These 
investments position us well to capitalize on the 
Biologics and Injectable opportunities across the 
domestic, Rest of World (RoW), and regulated mark ets. 
Whil e the external envir onment will continue to evol ve, 
our focus on disciplined execution, operational 
excellence, and high margin/ cashﬂow businesses 
remains unchanged. I extend my sincer e gratitude to 
our shareholders for their continued trust, to our 
customers and business partners for their enduring 
conﬁdence, and to our empl oyees for their passion, 
resilience, and commitment. T ogether , we remain well 
positioned to create lasting value for all our 
stakehol ders and to build an institution that we can all 
take pride in.
Warm Regar ds,
Amit Bakshi
Chairman and Managing Director

13 Annual R eport 2025-2026Dear Shar ehol ders,
FY26 was a year deﬁned by disciplined execution and 
progress in strengthening the Company’s growth 
platform. The business achieved a consolidated 
operating revenue of Rs. 3,129 crore with an operating 
proﬁt of Rs. 1,120 crore and operating mar gin of 35.8%. 
This represents a growth of 60 bps in operating mar gin 
yoy, which is the outcome of our continued focus on 
enhancing ﬁeld-force productivity, improving the 
quality of business mix, and harnessing operating 
leverage. Excluding the one-time impact from the Trade 
Generic s business that was discontinued in the year , the 
consolidated operating proﬁt mar gin stood at 37.50%, 
a benchmark for the Indian pharma industr y.
Our Domestic Branded Formulations business 
recorded a revenue of `2,778 crore in FY26, 
representing year -on-year growth of 11%, whil e EBITD A 
mar gin expanded by 50 basis points to 37%. Our Insulin 
and GLP -1 franchise was the strongest growth driver , 
delivering 32% growth during the year , signiﬁcantl y 
outperforming the CVM growth of 6.7%. Our 
Dermatol ogy business also continued to consistentl y 
outpace the mark et, recor ding FY26 growth of 14.2% 
compar ed with CVM growth of 8.6%. Both businesses 
have also delivered substantial improvements in 
proﬁtability since their respective acquisitions. The 
Biocon business achieved an operating mar gin of over 
30% in FY26, up from 19% at the time of acquisition, 
while the Dermatology business expanded its 
operating mar gin to over 40%, compar ed with 10% at 
acquisition. 
Our CVD business has shown impr ovement quarter on 
quarter with Q4 -FY26 gr owth being nearl y on par with 
its CVM growth. Our OHA business took a revenue loss 
of ~ Rs. 40+ crore during the year due to the ban on a 
signiﬁcant FDC (Glimisave MV 2.3) and is expected to 
recover its growth trajector y in the quarters to come.   
Following a delay of over a year, we successfully 
commenced the commer cial manuf acturing of insulin 
vials at our Bhopal f acilit y in August 2025, marking an 
import ant milestone in our insulin journey . We are also 
on track to commer cialise insulin cartridges from the 
facility in August 2026, further strengthening our 
integrated manufacturing capabilities. The 
commissioning of this facility positions us to fully 
capit alize on the Indian insulin opportunit y. Over the 
past two years, suppl y constraint s from third-part y 
manuf actur ers had resul ted in a revenue loss of Rs. 
100+ crore in insulins alone. With in-house manuf acturing now operational, we have signiﬁcantl y 
enhanced control over product quality and supply 
reliabilit y, whil e also impr oving the economic s of the 
insulin busine ss. This inve stment provide s a strong 
foundation for sustained growth and increased 
competitiveness in one of our most important 
therapeutic segments.
On the back of several lucrative mark et opportunities in 
India and overseas, we decided to front-load our capit al 
investment plans of Rs.~400 crore during the year , 
primaril y along three fronts.
 • Bhopal facilit y expansion: Vials ~ 130+ million p.a.
  and cartridges ~ 75 million p.a. – investment of Rs.
  150+ crore .
 • Steril e inject ables expansion: A third (greenﬁel d)
  unit with signiﬁcant capacit y addition in liquid
  ampoules, general dry powder injections and
  PFS / Lyo injections – investment of . Rs. 135 crore
 • Levim Lifetech: Clinical development of our
  biologics pipeline including Teriparatide,
  Romiplostim, Degludec, Degludec-Liraglutide
  combination and recombinant Semaglutide –
  follow on investment of . Rs. 100 crore
Our international business deliver ed a revenue of Rs. 
348 crore in the year with a 7% yoy growth on the back of 
suppl y chain disruptions in the middl e east. Following 
an EU-GMP inspection of our two steril e inject able 
manuf acturing units in March 2026, we received a set of 
non-compliance observations which are primarily 
procedural in natur e. As a resul t, the commencement of 
our EU CDMO revenue, originall y schedul ed for Q1 
FY27, is expected to be delayed. Remediation measur es 
are being impl emented at a good pace, and we expect 
to be able to request a reinspection in the coming 
months.
As we move into the next phase of our journey , our 
operating priorities remain clear - expanding 
technol ogy capabilities, enhancing qualit y systems and 
driving operational excellence. Whil e we continue to 
navigate a dynamic macr oeconomic and regulator y 
envir onment, I am conﬁdent that the commitment of 
our team and the trust of our customers will enabl e us to 
achieve our business objectives. 
Warm Regar ds,
Krishnakumar V
Executive Director & Chief Operating Ofﬁcer

15 Annual R eport 2025-2026Greetings. We continued to build on our core execution 
themes of operating leverage and mar gin expansion 
during FY26. The business clocked a consolidated 
revenue of Rs. 3,129 crore in FY26, representing a 8% 
growth yoy. EBID TA for the year stood at Rs. 1,120 crore, 
representing a growth of 10% and an expansion in 
operating mar gin f rom 35% in F Y25 to 35.8% in F Y26. 
Consolidated Proﬁt After Tax from operations stood at 
Rs. 498 crore with a 34% growth yoy. Reported Proﬁt 
After Tax stood at Rs. 648 crore on the back of a one-off 
upside of Rs. 150 crore from deferred tax adjustment. 
Our ﬂagship Domestic Branded Formulations (DBF ) 
business delivered a revenue of INR 2,778 crore, 
registering a YoY growth of 11%. The business segment 
generated an EBID TA of Rs. 1,026 crore for the year , 
representing a yoy gr owth of 12%. Our continued f ocus 
on operational l everage l ed to an expansion in EBID TA 
mar gin from 36.5% in FY25 to 37% in FY26. 
Swiss Parenterals contributed Rs. 348 crore in revenue 
with a 7% YoY growth on the back of sever e disruptions 
in international shipping channels. The segment 
EBID TA came in at Rs. 110 crore with an operating 
mar gin of 32%. The business experienced a mar gin 
compr ession from 33% in FY25 to 32% in FY26 due to 
investment (ahead of revenue) in its regulated mark ets 
CDMO expansion. The business continues to operate 
with strong return ratios of 30% ROCE and 28% ROE on 
a standal one basis.
Consolidated Gross Margin for the year came in at 
74.6%, lower by ~ 70 bps compar ed to FY25. This 
reﬂects the evol ving business mix of the company as 
well as the delay in commencement of insulin 
manufacturing from our Bhopal facility. This 
compr ession in Gross Margin was mor e than offset by 
operational leverage, resulting in a consolidated 
EBID TA mar gin of 35.8% in FY26, higher by ~ 60 bps 
from 35.2% in FY25. Consolidated OCF-to-EBIDTA 
conversion stood at 48% in FY26 relative to 105% in 
FY25, and hence in line with our long- term average of 
75%.Depr eciation expense stood at Rs. 55 crore, down by 
38% yoy and Inter est expense stood at Rs. 193 crore, 
down by 17% yoy. Deal -related amortisation stood at 
Rs. 225 crore, largely like that reported in FY25. Book 
tax rate for FY26 (excluding the impact of deferred tax 
adjustment ) stood at 22%, down by 140 bps from 23.4% 
in FY25. With a greater proportion of manuf acturing 
activit y to be brought into our Bhopal and Ahmedabad 
facilities, we expect to see a further reduction in book 
tax rate in the next few years. 
Capit al Expenditur e of ~ Rs. 2 97 crore was undert aken 
in FY26, primaril y towards our Biologic s and Inject ables 
operations. This is part of the Rs. 750-800 crore 
investment plan outlined by the Company over FY26-
FY28.
As we look ahead, we are conﬁdent in our abilit y to 
deliver signiﬁcant value to our shar ehol ders with an 
exponential impr ovement in EPS and ROCE over the 
coming years.
Warm Regar ds,
Sachin Shah
Chief Financial Ofﬁcer

17 Annual R eport 2025-2026Eris Lif escience s Ltd. is a publicl y 
listed pharmaceutical company 
and a l eader in the Dome stic 
Branded F ormulations mark et. 
Ranked among the Top-20 cos in 
the Indian Pharmaceutical 
Mark et (IPM), we r emain the 
younge st company t o featur e in 
this lis t. We continue t o hol d a 
strong mark et position in k ey 
special ties such as Insulins, GLP -
1, Or al Anti-Diabe tes, 
Dermat ology, CVD and VMN and 
our emer ging ther apie s 
consis ting of Oncol ogy, 
Nephrology and Women’s Health.
As we compl ete 19 years of focused growth and impact, we 
take pride in what we have built — and even mor e in wher e we 
are headed. From inception, we set out to be a company 
pivoted around special ty and super -special ty therapies and 
committed to science -led, outcome -driven heal thcar e, and 
that ambition has only strengthened as we scale our 
presence in chronic and compl ex diseases. 
FY26 was a deﬁning year in our evolution as we strengthened 
our play in Insulins and successfully entered the GLP-1 
mark et, which is a large opportunit y that opened up for 
generic competition in March-26. We have fully integrated 
the Biocon India Branded Formulations acquisition, 
strengthened our leadership in Insulins (RHI + Glar gin) with 
market share nearly doubled from 9% to 16% since 
acquisition. Initially meant to be a vertical integration 
strategy for our Insulin business, the Eris BioNxt facilit y is now 
well positioned to evol ve into a proﬁt centr e in its own right. 
Our initial strategic investment  in Levim Lifetech,  now being augmented by a second wave of investments in Diabesit y 
inject ables and DS manuf acturing, is helping us create an 
end- to-end platf orm from bulk recombinant biologic s to 
ﬁnished dosage f or Insulin Anal ogs and r ecombinant GLP- 1, 
that are progressing through pre-clinical and clinical 
milestones for launches over FY28–F Y29.
Our focused portf olio of power brands continues to anchor 
the franchise, with our Top-20 mother brands contributing 
around 60% of Domestic Branded Formulations revenue and 
six brands now above the INR 100 crore mark and ten in the 
INR 50–100 crore range. Starting from scratch in FY23, our 
pipeline of innovative Oral Solid Dose combinations has 
grown to 25-plus candidates across a wide range of 
therapies.
Swiss Parenterals, our international arm, is at the threshol d 
of a step-change. Since acquisition we have repositioned the 
busine ss towards higher- entr y-barrier RoW mark ets and 
CDMO partnerships. We expect this business to take a step 
jump in FY28 with the commer cialisation of a brand-new 
general inject ables facilit y (Unit -3), which will enabl e us to 
deepen our presence in strategic RoW mark ets like Latin 
America, MENA and CIS and also start commer cialising the 
CDMO contract pipeline. 
Starting with an Oral Solids unit at Guw ahati, we today 
operate six globall y accr edited and compliant manuf acturing 
facilities across India and covering a wide spectrum of 
technol ogies in small molecul es and biologics — from Oral 
Solids, liquids and topicals to steril e inject ables, biosimilar 
ﬁll-ﬁnish and recombinant insulin and GLP- 1 platf orms. 
In FY26, we deliver ed consolidated operating revenue of INR 
3,129 crores, registering a growth of 8% with an EBITD A of 
INR 1,120 crores and PAT of INR 648 crore. We continue to 
maintain industry leading margins with average Gross 
mar gin of 81%, EBITD A mar gin of 35% and Avg. Operating 
cash conversion ratio of 75-76% since IPO.
The last few years have been transf ormative. We remain 
deeply focused on executing our strategic priorities in 
Diabesit y, Special ty Injectabl es, Biologics and international 
markets, while upholding the core pillars of science, 
compliance, patient care and capit al discipline that deﬁne 
Eris — and creating sust ained value for all stakehol ders in the 
decades ahead.

A real-worl d mul ticent er cr oss-sectional obser vational s tudy t o
asse ss the clinical pr oﬁle of peripher al neur opathy in patient s with

Participants scr eened: 4,290 using a cr oss-sectional,
retrospective obser vational method, f ocusing on 
signs and symptoms of f oot complications and 
neur opathy
Published in PLOS ONE 2025; 20( 4): e0312085This study aimed to investigate the prevalence, 
characteristics, and risk factors for foot deformities in 
Asian-Indian individuals with type 2 diabetes mellitus 
(T2DM) and diabetic peripheral neur opathy (DPN).Dry skin foot (44%), infection (19.7%), and ingrown toenails 
(16.6%) were the most prevalent foot health 
complic ations, whil e burning (35.4%), muscl e cramps  
(31.5%), and loss of sensation (26.2%) were the most 
common neuropathic symptoms. Diabetic nephropathy 
(OR 3.96), diabetic retinopathy (OR 3.85), coronary artery 
disease (OR 3.48), COVID-19 history (OR 2.37), smoking 
(OR 2.13), hypertension (OR 2.10), dyslipidemia (OR 2.09), 
alcohol use (OR 1.57), and higher HbA1c (OR 1.29) were 
signiﬁcant predictors of multiple foot health 
complications. The study highlights the need for early foot 
screening and comprehensive diabetes management in 
Indian patients to prevent neuropathy-related deformities 
and complications.
A Randomiz ed, Mul ticent er Study Ev aluating the E fﬁcac y, Saf ety and
Tolerabilit y of D apagliﬂ ozin + Gliclazide Fix ed Dose C ombination
Over D apagliﬂ ozin Mono ther apy in T ype 2 Diabe tes Mellitus P atient s
Inadequat ely Contr olled on Me tformin Mono ther apy
Participants scr eened: 270; Participants
randomized: 244
Published in Cureus 2026 Feb 15; 18(2):e10367 6. This phase III study evaluated the efﬁcacy , safety, and 
tolerability of ﬁxed-dose combination (FDC) 
dapagliﬂ ozin 10 mg + gliclazide SR 60 mg compar ed 
with dapagliﬂozin 10 mg monotherapy in T2DM 
patients inadequately controlled on metformin 
monotherapy .At 16 weeks, the FDC showed signiﬁcantly greater 
reduction in HbA1c than monotherapy , with mean HbA1c 
falling from 8.76% to 6.97% versus 8.80% to 7.32%, and 
mean HbA1c change of 1.79% versus 1.48% (p = 0.039). 
Fasting plasma glucose reduction was 52.72 mg/dL 
versus 45.15 mg/dL (p = 0.008), and postprandial 
glucose reduction was 74.75 mg/ dL versus 65.90 mg/ dL 
(p = 0.038 ) in favor of the FDC. HbA1c below 8% was 
achieved in 81.9% patients in the FDC arm versus 76.2% 
in the monotherapy arm, and the combination was well 
tolerated with only 4.1% hypogl ycemic events and no 
serious adverse events. These ﬁndings establish 
dapagliﬂ ozin + gliclazide SR FDC as an effective and 
practical treatment option for uncontrolled T2DM 
patients in India

23 Annual R eport 2025-2026QRISK3-Based Car diovascular Risk Asse ssment Among Indian
Heal thcar e Professionals
Participants scr eened: 9,804  using Cr oss-sectional 
anal ysis
Published in European Journal of Cardiov ascular
Medicine 2025; 15( 11): 4 23–430This study aimed to quantif y 10-year cardiovascular 
disease risk using QRISK3 in Indian healthcare 
professionals, identif y predictors of high risk, compar e 
QRISK3 with other risk calculators, and examine 
regional variation.Mean QRISK3 was 11.0%, and 15.7% of healthcare 
professionals were classiﬁed as high risk; the cohort was 
predominantly male (91.6%) with a mean age of 52.3 
years. The strongest predictors of high cardiovascular risk 
were chronic kidney disease (OR 90.4), heavy smoking (OR 
27.9), atrial ﬁbrillation (OR 18.9), and rheumatoid arthritis 
(OR 7.2), while women had substantially lower risk than 
men (4.0% vs 11.6%). QRISK3 yielded higher risk estimates 
than ASCVD and up-classiﬁed 23% of participants, while 
regional analysis showed the highest adjusted risk in East 
India and the lowest in West India. The study supports 
routine cardiovascular risk assessment in healthcare 
professionals and careful choice of risk prediction tools in 
Indian settings.
This s tudy aims t o de termine the pr evalence of Micr oalbuminuria

Participants screened: 63,570 (T2DM patients

Published in the Interna tional Journal of Diabete s
and T echnol ogy 2025; 4: 1-4This study aims to determine the prevalence of 
Microalbuminuria (MAU) and identify clinical 
predictors in type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM) patientsMAU prevalence was 36.9% (n = 17,275). Key predictors 
included hemoglobin A1c (odds ratio [OR] = 1.18, P < 0.001), 
diabetes duration (OR = 1.04, P < 0.001), hypertension 
duration (OR = 1.03, P < 0.001), and diastolic BP (OR = 1.01, 
P < 0.001). Four out of ten patients with type 2 diabetes 
suffered from MAU in our study. Hence, strongly 
recommended to screen every diabetes patient in clinical 
practice to detect early signs of kidney damage.

Patient s in India
Participants scr eened: 494 2
Published in the The Journal of Clinical Hyper tension,
2025; 27 :e14963The study evaluated initial anti-hypertensive drug 
prescription patterns in Indian healthcar e settings and 
investigated the extent to which physicians adher ed to 
either European or Indian hypertension guidelines.Angiotensin Receptor Blocker (ARB) i.e. Telmisartan was 
the preferred initial therapy , followed by calcium channel 
blockers (cilnidipine and amlodipine). The combination 
of ARBs with CCBs was the most frequentl y prescribed 
regimen. Beta blockers were prescribed as a ﬁrst line 
treatment in 15% of patients without a clear indication. 
This suggests that most physicians follow the ESH 
guidelines; however, the high initial monotherapy 
prescription of ARB and CCB suggests adher ence to the 
Indian guidelines. However, a signiﬁcant number of 
patients did not receive appropriate treatment, 
highlighting areas for improvement in prescription 
practices.

28Annual R eport 2025-2026ABPM on Call
Our ﬂagship initiative, Ambulator y Blood Pressur e Monitoring (ABPM) on Call, is designed to 
raise awareness regar ding the critical import ance of 24-hour blood pressur e management. This 
portable, non-invasive device allows individuals to continue their daily routines without 
disruption whil e capturing a holistic view of their blood pressur e patterns, offering mor e 
compr ehensive clinical insights than conventional clinic-based measur ements. To date, this 
initiative has successfull y screened over 188,000 patients, signiﬁcantl y enhancing the precision 
of hypertension management.
Holter on Call
This program focuses on the signiﬁcance of Ambulatory Electrocardiogram (AECG) monitoring, 
which enables the continuous recording of heart activity over an extended period—typically 24 to 48 
hours or longer. By offering prolonged monitoring, AECG enhances the probability of detecting 
intermittent arrhythmias that might go unnoticed in a standard clinical electrocardiogram, 
ultimately supporting better clinical decisions and patient quality of life. To date, this initiative has 
successfully screened over 100,000 patients, reinforcing our commitment to effective cardiac care.
CGM on Call
Continuous Glucose Monitoring (CGM) on Call provides a near real-time, compr ehensive view of 
glucose trends for individuals with diabetes. This technol ogy empowers clinicians to mak e 
informed adjustments to medication dosages, administration timings, diet, and physical activit y, 
all of which are essential for enhancing glycemic contr ol. As a vital tool for both patients and 
healthcar e professionals, CGM plays a crucial role in personaliz ed diabetes management. To 
date, this initiative has succe ssfull y screene d over 75,000 patient s, contributing to mor e 
proactive and effective diabetes care.
PMOScareclinicsSleep device on Call
The medical communit y is increasingl y recognizing the critical link between cardiovascular 
diseases and sleep disor ders. Eris supports clinicians in diagnosing sleep apnea, interpr eting 
resul ts, devel oping treatment plans, and facilit ating essential patient counseling. To date, this 
initiative has successfull y screened over 36,000 patients for sleep-r elated issues, enhancing the 
clinical understanding and management of cardiac conditions to ensur e impr oved patient care.
Montana ANC Associa te
Guided by the Fetal Origin of Adult Diseases (FOAD) hypothesis—which suggests that events during 
early development, particularly in utero, increase the risk of chronic diseases in adulthood—this 
FOGSI-Eris initiative is dedicated to bridging diagnostic gaps in conditions like anemia, pregnancy-
induced hypertension (PIH), and gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM). By offering point-of-care 
diagnostic support, this initiative strengthens antenatal care and helps prevent the long-term onset 
of chronic adult diseases. To date, Montana ANC Associate has successfully screened over 200,000 
patients, reinforcing our impact on maternal and fetal health.
PCOS Care Clinic
We initiated the PCOS Care Clinic to promote early screening, timely diagnosis, and 
compr ehensive management of Polycystic Ovar y Syndr ome. Through this program, patients are 
educated about the import ance of lifestyle modiﬁcation, metabolic monitoring, and appr opriate 
medical management to r educe l ong- term risks such as inf ertilit y, diabetes, and car diovascular 
complications. To date, we have screened mor e than 2,000 patients through these clinics.
Sparsh Camp  (Neur otouch)
This initiative raises awareness about the importance of early neuropathy screening, as 
symptoms are often subtl e and overl ooked until signiﬁcant —sometimes irreversibl e—ner ve 
damage occurs. Sparsh Camps facilit ate early diagnosis through the use of a port able, non-
invasive screening device capabl e of detecting both small and large ﬁber neur opathy . The 
screening includes compr ehensive assessments such as vibration perception, thermal sensitivit y, 
monoﬁlament tests, and skin temperatur e evaluation, helping neur ologists identif y neur opathy 
at an early, treatabl e stage. Over the past year , mor e than 32,500 patients have been screened 
through this program.

BUSINESS
Swiss Parenterals, our international business platform, 
continued to mak e strategic progress during FY26 despite a 
few near -term headwinds. Our international presence now 
spans 70+ countries across Latin America, Africa, Asia-
Paciﬁc, the Middl e East and CIS mark ets, with the business 
increasingl y positioned to participate in higher -entr y-barrier 
regulated mark ets.
A key milestone during the year was the receipt of ANVISA 
approval in August 2025 for both injectable facilities, following the April 2025 inspections, which paves the way for 
entr y into Brazil. Following an EU-GMP inspection of our two 
steril e inject able manuf acturing units in March 2026, we 
received a set of non-compliance obser vations which are 
primaril y procedural in natur e. Remediation measur es are 
being impl emented at a good pace, and we expect to be able 
to request a reinspection in the coming months. The 
commer cialization of our CDMO business has been delayed 
pending remediation of the EU obser vations in the two 
facilities.

INFORMA TION TECHNOL OGY Eris continues to position information technol ogy as a core 
enabl er of busine ss growth, operational excellence, and

foundation - comprising enterprise applications, cloud

Management System (ISMS ). This governance framework 
ensur es the ethical handling of data, stringent compliance, 
and operational resilience.
During the year, the Company accelerated its digital 
transformation journey through several strategic milestones:
 • Enterprise Core: Successful rollout of SAP S/4HANA
  (Project LEAP) as the digital core, enabling faster
  decision-making, improve d resource management,
  and standar dised processes across the organisation
 • Workplace & Process Efﬁciency: Migration to Micr osof t
  Ofﬁce 365 enhanced secure collaboration and
  communication. Concurrently, the introduction of
  Robotic Proce ss Automation (RPA) through Power
  Automate signiﬁcantl y reduced manual inter vention
  and minimised operational errors
 • HR Digitalisation: The full implementation of Darwinbox
  HRMS streamlined the empl oyee experience, from
  onboarding to performance management, while
  providing advanced anal ytics for leadership 
 • Digital Innovation: Eris expanded its digital engagement
  through immersive technologies, including AR/VR  and holography for doctor engagement, alongside
  digital health initiatives that integrate wearable-
  based solutions (e.g., Continuous Glucose Monitoring )
  with AI-driven insights
 • Infrastructure & Security: A cloud-ﬁrst, lean data
  centr e strategy was adopted to impr ove scalabilit y and
  cost-efﬁciency. Cybersecurity was further fortiﬁed

At Eris Lifesciences, we are committed to building long- term 
value through innovation, responsibilit y, and patient -centric 
solutions. Guided by our purpose of transf orming heal thcar e 
for better outcomes, we integrate Envir onment al, Social, and 
Governance (ESG) principles into our strategy and 
operations. We remain steadf ast in our commitment to a 
futur e that creates long- term value for all our stak ehol ders 
wher e heal thcar e and sust ainabilit y conver ge. By adopting 
sustainable practices and environmentally responsible 
processes into our operations, we aim to reduce our 
ecological impact and ensure prudent resource 
management to support long-term sustainable growth. 
During the year , we have continued to maint ain a framework 
that guides our perf ormance on those ESG principl es that are 
most impactful for our business.
MATERIALIT Y ASSESSMENT
In FY25, Eris volunt arily engaged an independent third-part y 
to undert ake a compr ehensive materialit y assessment. The 
exercise was designed to identif y and prioritise ESG issues 
that are most signiﬁcant to its business operations and 
stakeholders. This was followed, drawing on globally 
accepted sust ainabilit y frameworks such as SASB and GRI, 
and benchmark ed against peers across different stages of 
ESG maturit y and independent global references.
1.  Stak ehol der Engagement and Anal ysis: 
  Prioritization of stak ehol ders based on their inﬂuence,
  impact, and relevance to Eris’s business and sustainability
  agenda. 2. Univ erse of ESG Topic s
  A compr ehensive list of material topics based on sector -
  speciﬁc ESG chall enges, risks, and opportunities, aligned
  with the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board
  (SASB) and Global Reporting Initiative (GRI).
3. Peer Benchmarking
  Benchmarking with peers with similar business models,
   operational footprints, and therapeutic areas across
  levels of ESG maturity as per global independent
  benchmarks f or a compr ehensive coverage of topics and
  underst anding of sectoral priorities. 
4. Evalua tion of Eris’s Busine ss Prioritie s 
  Survey of department and functional leaders followed by
  interactions to deﬁne sector and company-speciﬁc
  chall enges, risks, opportunities and growth ambitions. 
5. Anal ysis of Material ESG Prioritie s 
  An analy sis of qualitative and quantitative input s to
  identif y key trends and concerns. 
The process culminated in a multi-stak ehol der workshop 
with executive and functional leadership across the 
organisation to ﬁnalise the relative priorities between 
material ESG issues. These were aligned with industr y best 
practices and emerging sustainability trends, risks and 
opportunities to reﬁne the material ESG priorities in the 
context of Eris’ s business.

Environmental responsibility remains a material ESG focus 
area for Eris. The Company continues to meet all 
environmental compliance requirements under applicable 
regulatory and statutory norms, supported by an empowered 
governance structure (detailed in Section B of the BRSR 
report) that drives performance across levels. Standalone 
performance is disclosed in greater detail under Principle 6 of 
the BRSR (Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report).
Occupational Health and Safety is an integral part of the 
Company’s Human Capital Management strategy. Eris 
recognizes the detriment al impact of safety incidents on 
employee and worker well-being and takes proactive 
measur es to ensur e that the highest standar ds are being 
adher ed to. Safety processes and practices are horiz ontall y 
depl oyed across our locations, and the Company aspir es to 
obtain additional certiﬁcation for its facilities over the due 
course of business in addition to its WHO- GMP status for all 
of its facilities.
ENER GY SAVINGS INITIA TIVES
We have activel y undert aken a range of initiatives at our 
Guwahati manufacturing facility focused on improving 
ener gy efﬁciency . Key ener gy-saving measur es impl emented 
include :
 • Continuous Energy Monitoring:  Ongoing tracking of
  energy consumption against operational requirements  allows for real-time adjustments, ensuring efﬁcient
  ener gy use.
 • Real-Time Power Factor Monitoring with Audibl e
  Alerts: This system ensur es that any failur e triggers
  an immediate audible alert, enabling prompt
  preventive action to avoid inefﬁciencies or equipment
  damage.
 • Hot Water Tank Insulation: Insulating hot water
  tanks has signiﬁcantl y reduced heat loss, contributing
  to overall ener gy conser vation.
 • Installation of Automatic Moisture Separators:
  Installed at all air receivers, these have effectivel y
  eliminated water layer formation on cartridge ﬁlters,
  ther eby enhancing the operational efﬁciency of air
  compr essors.
 • Preventive Maintenance Programs: Regularly
  scheduled maintenance of HVAC systems, plant
  machinery, and utility equipment has been
  instrumental in maintaining optimal energy
  efﬁciency .
Similar ener gy management and efﬁciency impr ovement 
opportunities are being identiﬁed at all our manuf acturing 
locations. At Eris’ s Ahmedabad facilit y, ener gy audits have 
been conducted to pinpoint speciﬁc process impr ovements 
which can positivel y impact ener gy consumption. Hot spots 
for ener gy consumption have been identiﬁed to help direct 
the efforts.

The Company has also continue d to adopt cleaner fuel 
alternatives as part of its broader ener gy transition efforts, 
including the replacement of LVFO (Low Viscosit y Fuel Oil), a 
fossil fuel, with biomass wooden pellets for use in the boiler. 
The biomass boiler commissioned at the Guw ahati plant 
during Q4 FY25 continues to support carbon reduction 
efforts. Biomass briquettes are also used at the Eris 
Ahmedabad facilit y as well as at both Swiss Parenterals 
facilities.

state -of-the-art Ahmedabad and Swiss Parenterals facilities 
for utilizing solar ener gy. The Company is further channelling 
investments towards solar ener gy adoption at its Bhopal 
facilit y.
WATER MANA GEMENT INITIA TIVES
At our Guwahati manufacturing facility, we operate an 
Efﬂuent Treatment Plant (ETP) incorporating a Zero Liquid 
Discharge (ZLD) process. This system is meticulously 
engineered to eliminate liquid waste, with the primary 
objective of minimizing wastewater generation while 
enabling the recover y of clean water for reuse in applications 
such as gardening, cooling towers, and other utility 
processes. We have also depl oyed ﬂow meters to enabl e 
continuous monitoring and effective control of water 
consumption across the suppl y lines.

efﬂuent and in addition, we also have rainw ater harvesting

60Annual R eport 2025-2026ECONOMIC O VER VIEW
GLOBAL
In 2025, the global econom y proved mor e resilien t than  
expected despite elevated trade tensions, policy uncertainty and 
geopolitical frictions. Growth was supported by robust private 
consumption, AI-related investment, strong production activity 
and accommodative ﬁnancial and ﬁscal conditions, even as 
supply chains adjusted to higher trade barriers. This stronger-
than-anticipated performance extended the ﬁve-year global 
recovery from the 2020 recession, one of the most sustained 
recoveries in more than six decades. However, the recovery 
remained uneven: while advanced economies broadly regained 
momentum, a signiﬁcant shar e of emer ging mark et and 
developing economies (EMDEs), particularly low-income and 
conﬂict-affected economies, continued to lag pre-pandemic 
income levels.
The start of 2026 has been mor e volatil e, with escalating 
conﬂict in the Middl e East increasing risks to ener gy mark ets, 
trade ﬂows and ﬁnancial conditions. Disruptions in the Strait 
of Hormuz and damage to regional ener gy infrastructur e 
have contributed to higher energy prices and added 
uncertainty to the global outlook. Although ﬁnancial 
conditions remain broadly accommodative, prolonged 
geopolitical stress may raise inﬂationary pressures and 
temper global growth momentum.
Looking ahead, Global GDP growth is projected to remain 
broadly stable at 3.1% in 2026 before edging up to 3.2% in 
2027, sustained by robust technology-related investment and 
gradually lower effective tariff rates. However, the evolving 
conﬂict in the Middle East weighs on growth and generates 
signiﬁcant uncertainty around global demand. These 
projections assume that the current energy market disruption 
is temporary, with prices easing from mid-2026 onward.Note: The ﬁgures show the projections for global GDP grow th and G20 
headline inﬂation from the December 2025 OECD Economic Outlook , the 
current March 2026 Interim Economic Outlook and a preliminar y updat e of the 
December 2025 Economic Outlook projections using revised data for 2025 
and monthly information up until the end of Februar y 2026. The preliminar y 
updat e has a revised assessment for the ﬁrst quart er of 2026, with quart er-on-
quart er grow th and inﬂation in subsequent quart ers set in line with those 
project ed in the December 2025 Outlook. Source: OECD Interim Economic 
Outlook 119 database; OECD Economic Outlook 118 database; and OECD 
calculations
Note: The series shown in Panel A are PPP-weight ed aggregat es covering 36 
advanced economies and 36 emerging-mark et economies. Source: OECD 
Consumer Price database; national sources; LSEG; and OECD calculations.
INDIA
In FY2026, Indian economy remained resilient with strong 
economic momentum and estimated to grow by 7.7% during 
the same period. This growth was supported by private 
consumption, with government consumption also 
contributing positivel y. Investment was sust ained by easing 
ﬁnancial conditions and continued public capit al spending. 
Manuf acturing output has grown steadil y, reﬂecting robust 
demand for capital goods and steady growth in exports of 
engineering products. The services sector was a major MANA GEMENT

GLOBAL
The global pharmaceutical industr y is navigating a compl ex 
envir onment shaped by scientiﬁc progress, demographic 
trends, and geopolitical factors that inﬂuence therapy 
development and delivery. Companies are accelerating 
innovation through biomedical research and curative 
treatments, whil e simul taneousl y expanding generics and 
biosimilars to impr ove access, affordabilit y, and heal thcar e 
system efﬁciency .
The mark et has expanded at a compound annual growth rate 
(CAGR) of around 8%, rising from USD 1,570 billion in 2021 to 
around USD 1,939 billion in 2025. Key factors driving this 
growth include an ageing population, the rising preval ence 
of chronic diseases robust development in the generics 
market, and the increasing consumer orientation of 
healthcar e, particularl y in the over the counter ( OTC) space, 
with increasing focus on personalised treatment and 
precision medicine, aiming to tailor medical care to 
individual patient characteristics, needs, preferences, and 
genetic proﬁles.
Going ahead, the mark et is projected to continue growing at 
a 5-8% CAGR over the next ﬁve years, reaching an estimated 
USD 2,630 to 2,660 billion by 2030
INDIA
The Indian Pharmaceuticals mark et clocked growth of 9.9% 
in FY26 majorl y on the back of price increase. As per IQVIA 
MAT March’26 data, chronic segment continued to 
outperf orm, growing at 13.8% vs acute segment growth of 
7.4%. Muted acute season was the primar y cause behind 
slow IPM growth. contributor to growth, led by robust perf ormance in ﬁnance, 
trade, transport, and information technology. Headline 
inﬂation fell to 0.3% in October 2025, driven by lower Goods 
and Services Tax (GST ) rate s, easing food price s, which 
constitute nearl y half of the CPI bask et, and lower ener gy 
costs. Core inﬂation remains around 4%.
However, the GDP growth was slightly moderated by 
bilateral tariffs imposed by the United States which weigh on 
exports and investment, especiall y in labour -intensive and 
export-oriented sectors such as textiles, chemicals, and 
engineering goods, later followed by escalating conﬂict in 
the Middl e East increasing risks to ener gy mark ets, trade 
ﬂows and ﬁnancial conditions. Despite these headwinds the 
economy growth remained strong with growth now projected 
6.8- 7.2% in FY27 .

Financial Highlight s
Revenue from Oper ations:
For the year under review, the Company’s consolidated 
Revenue from Operations grew by 8.1% to INR 31,294 mn 
from INR 28,936 mn in FY 25. The Domestic Branded 
Formulations revenue grew by 11% to INR 27,776 mn.
Gross Proﬁt:
The consolidated gross margin stood at 75% in FY26 
representing a 7% growth yoy and a mar gin compr ession of 
75 bps on account of signiﬁcant changes in product/ 
business mix. With a gradual uptick in the scale of insour cing 
manufacturing operations (of the portfolios acquired in 
previous years), we expect further upside in gross mar gins 
going forward.Empl oyee Expense s: 
Empl oyee Expenses were up by 10%. 
Other Expense s: 
The consolidated other expenses in FY26 were INR 6,569 
mn, remained ﬂat in FY26. However , as a % of sales it is down 
by 173 bps. 
Depre ciation and Amor tisation:
Consolidated Depr eciation decr eased from INR 885 mn in 
FY25 to INR 547 mn in FY26. Amortisation decr eased from 
INR 2,27 0 mn in FY25 to INR 2,248 mn in FY26, owing to 
addition of acquisition related intangibl e expenses during 
the year .
Finance Expense s and Other Income:
Consolidated Finance Cost decr eased from INR 2,313 mn in 
FY25 to INR 1,927 mn in FY26. We expect the ﬁnance cost to 
further decr ease on account of debt reduction during the 
year . Consolidated Other Income decr eased from INR 184 mn 
in FY25 to INR 78 mn in FY26.
Earnings before Depre ciation, Amor tisation, Intere st and 
Taxes (EBITD A): 
Consolidated EBITD A stood at INR 11,201 mn, representing a 
36% margin and expansion of ~64 bps over last year. 
Domestic Branded Formulations EBITD A mar gin expanded 
from 36.5% in FY25 to 37.0% in FY26; driven by syner gies 
from business integration. 
 
Tax Expense s: 
Our effective book tax rate decr eased to 22% (Excluding DTL 
impact ) in FY26 from 23% in FY25, down by 140 bps
Proﬁt After Tax (PAT): 
Our Consolidated reported Proﬁt After Tax (PAT) for the year 
stood at INR 6,475 mn, a 73% YoY growth due to One -off 
upside Rs. 1,500 mn from deferred tax adjustment in PAT. 
Normalised PAT excluding the impact of Deferred Tax was 
Rs. 4,975 mn, a growth of 33% driven by disciplined cost 
management and a reduction in ﬁnance and tax expenses

Risk and Risk Mitiga tion
The Company has established a robust Enterprise Risk 
Management (ERM) framework to identif y, assess, monitor , 
and mitigate risks that may impact on the achievement of its 
strategic objectives. Oversight of the framework is provided 
by the Risk Management Committee, which periodicall y 
reviews the Company's risk proﬁle, key mitigation measur es, 
and emer ging risks.
The ERM framework covers strategic, operational, ﬁnancial, 
regulator y, compliance, qualit y, suppl y chain, information 
securit y, cybersecurit y, business continuit y, and reputational 
risks. Risk management is embedded into business planning 
and decision-making processes through structured risk 
assessments, deﬁned risk ownership, and ongoing 
monitoring.
The Company continuously evaluates changes in the 
business and regulatory environment and proactively 
impl ements mitigation measur es to enhance resilience and 
safeguard stakeholder interests. Internal audit reviews, 
busine ss continuit y planning, and appr opriate insurance 
coverage further strengthen the overall risk management 
framework.
The Boar d and management remain committed to fostering 
a strong risk-aware cultur e and continuousl y enhancing risk 
management capabilities to support sust ainabl e growth and 
long- term value creation.Outl ook
STRENGTHS AND OPP ORTUNITIES
Strong brands: The Company continues to focus on 
maint aining the strength of its brand portf olio; the Top 25 
Mother Brands contribute ~ 65% of its Branded 
Formulations revenue. With 13 out of these 25 brands being 
rank ed among the Top 5 in their respective segments, they 
are well poised for strong futur e growth. The Company has 6 
mother brands with revenues of Rs. 100+ crore p.a. and 10 
mother brands with revenues of Rs. 50 – 100 crore p.a. The 
Company ’s strong portf olio of Mother Brands enjoys leading 
prescription ranks with their respective doctor special ties. 
The Company holds a dominant position in key special ty 
areas such as Diabetol ogists, Cardiologists, Neur ologists, 
Gynecol ogists, Dermatol ogists and Consul ting Physicians.
Expansion in Emerging Specialtie s: The company derives 
48% of Branded Formulations revenue from eight emer ging 
special ties of Dermatol ogy, Insulins, W omen’ s Heal th, CNS, 
Oncol ogy, Critical Care, Gastr o and Nephr ology, giving us 
signiﬁcant headroom for disruptive growth in these 
segments.
Patent Expiration Opportunities: A number of key 
molecul es in the cardiomet abolic segment are expected to 
go off-patent over the next 3–5 years. Given our established 
position in this mark et, Eris is well placed to captur e these 
opportunities, as reﬂected in the strong franchise positions 
we have built over the last few years.
New Business Segments: The acquisition of Swiss 
Parenterals Ltd. and Biocon’s domestic branded formulations 
business have provided us with a strong platform to deepen 
our presence in the Indian injectables market. In addition, 
leveraging Swiss Parenterals’ distribution network in semi-
regulated markets will help us build an oral solid dosage 
business in these geographies and expand our addressable 
market.

THRE ATS, RISKS AND CONCERNS
Pandemic-lik e Situation:  The outbr eak of the COVID- 19 like 
pandemic across the worl d and subsequent disruption in 
economic activities apart from impacting GDP across 
countrie s also cause s various disruptions which impacts 
demand of pharmaceutical products.Regulator y Overhang: The Drug Price Contr ol (Amendment ) 
Order limits price increases in schedul e drugs mentioned in 
the National List of Essential Medicines (NLEM). Whil e it has 
been obser ved that competitive forces in the mark et have 
been mor e effective in contr olling prices, amendments in the 
list will continue to pose chall enges for the industr y. As on 
31st March 2026, 17% of the Company ’s revenue was from 
drugs schedul ed in the NLEM (vs. 17% for the Indian Pharma 
Mark et). 
GOING AHE AD
Eris’ s strong current portf olio will further reap beneﬁts of 
investments in pr oducts, pr omotions, pr ocesses and peopl e 
in last coupl e of years. Whil e the existing business remains 
the main engine of growth for the Company , in-licensing, 
expl oring newer therapeutic areas and futur e patent expiries 
are some areas that it will leverage for future growth. 
Productivit y is also expected to impr ove further with further 
expansion in doctor reach.
The C ompany is conﬁdent to continue to outperf orm in cor e 
therapies as a result of super specialit y focus, strong power 
brands, presence in fast growing molecul es and adapt abilit y 
and resilience of the business model.

2. STATE OF AFFAIRS (standalone): 
• The  total revenue for the financial year under review was Rs. 1,920.05 crore as against Rs. 1,733.26 crore in the previous 
year, recording a growth of 10.78%

financial year, recording a growth of 215.35%.   
3. DIVIDEND:
 The Board of Directors have considered it prudent not to recommend any dividend on the equity shares of the Company for 
the financial year ended March 31, 2026. During the previous financial year, the Company had declared and paid an interim 
dividend of Rs. 7.35 per equity share (at the rate of 735%) on the equity shares of the Company, with no final dividend.
 Further, in accordance with the Company’s Dividend Distribution Policy, the Board had declared and the Company paid an 
interim dividend of Rs. 7.21 per equity share (at the rate of 721%) during the current financial year 2026–27, while maintaining 
an appropriate balance between shareholder returns and the Company’s long-term financial and operational requirements.
 DIVIDEND DISTRIBUTION POLICY
 Dividend Distribution Policy of your Company aims at striking the right balance between the quantum of dividend paid to 
its Shareholders and the amount of profits retained for its business requirements, present and future. The Policy intends to 
broadly specify various external and internal factors that shall be considered while declaring dividend, the circumstances 
under which the Shareholders of the Company may or may not expect dividend, the financial parameters that shall be 
considered while declaring dividend and the parameters that shall be adopted with regard to various classes of shares. 
       The Company has adopted the Dividend Distribution Policy and the said policy is available on the website of the Company at 
https://www.eris.co.in/investor-relations/corporate-governance/policies

with the provisions of Regulation 17(1) of SEBI LODR Regulations. 
 Explanation from Board of Directors of the Company: 
 There was a delay in the appointment of an Independent Director due to the time involved in identification, evaluation, due 
diligence, approvals, and completion of the appointment process to ensure selection of a suitable and qualified candidate in 
the long-term interest of the Company and its stakeholders. 
 The Company prioritised prudent selection and governance of the highest standard over any name sake compliance by means 
of any rushed appointment.

29. DISCLOSURE ON ESTABLISHMENT OF VIGIL MECHANISM:
 The Company provides an avenue to the Directors and Employees of the Company to report without fear any instance of an 
actual or suspected violation, wrongdoings, or any illegal or unethical, or improper practice which may adversely impact the 
image and/or the financials of the Company. For this, the Company has in place a Vigil Mechanism Policy (Whistle Blower 
Policy) for Directors and employees to report genuine concerns. 
=== page 79 ===
78 This provides for adequate safeguards against the victimization of employees and Directors who wish to use the vigil 
mechanism to bring any wrong deed(s) to the notice of the Company.
 During the financial year under review, the implementation of the vigil mechanism has been properly and regularly monitored 
by the Audit Committee. However, no complaints or instances in this regard have been reported in the financial year 2025-26. 
The said policy is available on the Company’s website at https://www.eris.co.in/investor-relations/corporate-governance

1.  During the preceding FY 2024-25, the turnover of the Company exceeded `300 crore and, accordingly, the Company was 
required to comply with the following provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 read with the applicable rules made thereunder:
• Pursuant to provisions of Section 149(4) of the Companies Act, 2013 read with Rule 4(2) of the Companies (Appointment 
and Qualification of Directors) Rules, 2014, appointment of at least two Independent Directors in the composition of the 
Board of Directors of the Company.
=== page 89 ===
88• Pursuant to provisions of Section 177 of the Companies Act, 2013, it is mandatory to have at least two Independent 
Directors in the composition of Audit Committee of the Company.
• Pursuant to provisions of Section 178 of the Companies Act, 2013, it is mandatory to have at least three Non-Executive 
Directors out of which not less than one half shall be Independent Directors in composition of Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee of the Company.
• Pursuant to provisions of Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013, it is mandatory to have to have at least one Independent 
Director on its CSR Committee of the Company.
 However, as the Company did not appoint any Independent Director therefore the above provisions were not complied with 
by the Company from April 1, 2025, to January 15, 2026.
 With effect from January 16, 2026, the Company became a wholly owned subsidiary of Eris Lifesciences Limited, pursuant to 
which the requirement to have an Independent Director on the above committees ceased to be applicable to the Company.
2. During the preceding financial year, i.e. FY 2024-25, the turnover of the Company exceeded `300 crore and, accordingly, 
pursuant to Section 149(1) read with Rule 3 of the Companies (Appointment and Qualification of Directors) Rules, 2014, the 
Company was required to have at least one Woman Director on its Board. However, the Company did not appoint a Woman 
Director during FY 2025-26 and, consequently, remained non-compliant with the aforesaid statutory requirement during the 
relevant period.
We further report that
During the year under review, the composition of Board of Directors of the Company is not duly constituted with proper balance 
of Directors as the Company was required to appoint at least two Independent Directors in the Board along with one woman 
Director pursuant to the provisions of Section 149 of the Companies Act, 2013 and the same were not appointed by the Company 
thus Company has not complied with the provisions of Section 149 of the Act. However, the required composition to the extent of 
appointment of two Independent Directors was not maintained only for the period from April 1, 2025, to January 15, 2026. With 
effect from January 16, 2026, the Company became a wholly owned subsidiary of Eris Lifesciences Limited, pursuant to which the 
requirement to appoint two independent directors ceased to apply.

90Annexure 2
PARTICULARS OF ENERGY CONSERVATION, TECHNOLOGY ABSORPTION AND FOREIGN EXCHANGE EARNINGS AND 
OUTGO REQUIRED UNDER THE COMPANIES (ACCOUNTS) RULES, 2014
Information as per Section 134(3)(m) of the Companies Act, 2013, read with Companies (Accounts) Rules, 2014 and forming part 
of the Directors’ Report to the members for the financial year ended on 31st March, 2026.
Conservation of energy:
Steps taken for conservation of energy:
• Power consumption monitoring is regularly done at the manufacturing facility, resulting in optimum energy consumption and 
conservation.
• Use of LED lights and human sensor switches to save electricity has been adopted.
• Regulation in usage of office lighting.
• Regular monitoring of high energy consumption areas and taking appropriate measures as and when required. For instance, 
optimisation of compressed air generation process, thermal insulation of hot water tanks and steam lines, preventive/periodic 
maintenance of HVAC plants and utility equipment.
• All efforts are made to use more natural lights in the premises to optimise the consumption of energy.
• Reduction in energy consumption for compressed air generation by placing an air reservoir to store and maintain air pressure.
• Hot water tanks have been insulated to minimize energy loss.
• Optimisation of energy efficiency through preventive maintenance activities of systems like HVAC, plant and utility equipment 
at regular intervals.
• Cladding of hot water line has been undertaken to prevent any energy loss.
The steps taken by the company for utilising alternate sources of energy:
• The Company evaluates all possibilities of utilizing alternate sources of energy in its operations, wherever possible.
The capital investment on energy conservation equipment:
• During the year, the company has not made any capital investment on energy conservation equipment.
Technology absorption:
• Company regularly monitors the technical advancements which can help in reducing cost and make the existing processes 
more eco-friendly and result in minimization of environmental hazards.
• The benefits derived from these efforts would be product improvement, cost reduction, and sustainable development.
• No technology was imported by the Company during the year under review.
• During the year, the Company has not made any expenditure on research and development related to technology absorption.

1. BRIEF OUTLINE ON COMPANY’S CSR POLICY:
 While the Company continues to expand and grow in its sector of business, it has not lost sight of its commitment to play 
its role as an enlightened corporate citizen. Corporate Social Responsibility has always been on its agenda. Pursuant to the 
provisions of Companies Act, 2013 and the Companies (Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) Rules, 2014 the Company has 
framed a CSR Policy which lays down guidelines for the Company to make CSR as one of the key focus areas and contribute 
to society through high impact, sustainable programmes by undertaking the activities in accordance with Schedule VII of the 
Companies Act, 2013.
The Company’s focus areas under CSR are:
• Preventive Medical Screening
• Education
• Environment
• Rural & Agricultural Development
2. THE COMPOSITION OF THE CSR COMMITTEE:
 The CSR committee of the Board is responsible, inter alia, for overseeing the execution of the Company’s CSR policy.

CORPORATE GOVERNANCE REPORT
(Pursuant to provisions of Schedule V of the SEBI (LODR) Regulations, 2015 / “SEBI Listing Regulations”)
1. COMPANY’S PHILOSOPHY ON CODE OF GOVERNANCE:
Our organization wholeheartedly embraces the immense importance of corporate governance. We recognize the essential role 
a strong corporate governance structure plays in ensuring the enduring prosperity and sustainability of our Company, as well 
as the welfare of all those impacted. At all levels of our organization, we give utmost priority to the core principles of openness, 
responsibility, and ethical conduct. By steadfastly adhering to best practices and fostering transparent lines of communication, 
we guarantee that our decision-making procedures remain equitable and unbiased. 
We proactively involve and take into account the perspectives of all stakeholders, protecting their interests and fostering a cli -
mate of confidence. Our commitment to corporate governance goes beyond mere adherence to rules; it is deeply ingrained in 
our corporate culture and acts as a guiding principle for our daily conduct. We understand that by upholding the most stringent 
corporate governance standards, we not only improve our own performance but also cultivate robust relationships with our em -
ployees, customers, partners, and communities.
2. BOARD OF DIRECTORS:
The Board of Directors holds a central position within an organization, playing a vital role in its achievements. The presence of 
an ideal blend of executive and independent directors is imperative, as it heightens the board’s efficacy, objectivity, and sincerity, 
thereby directly benefiting the investors. This well-balanced composition of Directors ensures a diverse range of perspectives and 
prevents biases.
Aligned with our dedication to this principle, our Board of Directors (Board) is composed of a total of 8 Directors, including 3 
Promoter Executive Directors, 1 Executive Director, and 4 Non-Executive Independent Directors. This composition fully adheres 
to the relevant provisions outlined in the Companies Act, 2013 (“the Act”), as well as the Securities and Exchange Board of India 
(SEBI) (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“Listing Regulations”). The Board is headed by an 
Executive Promoter Director.
None of the Directors on the Board hold directorships in more than ten public companies. Further in accordance with Regulation 
26(1) of Listing Regulations, none of them is a member of more than ten committees or Chairman of more than five committees 
across all the public companies in which he/she is a Director. Necessary disclosures regarding Committee positions in other 
public companies as of March 31, 2026, have been made by the Directors.
Independent Directors are non-executive directors as defined under Regulation 16(1)(b) of the SEBI Listing Regulations read 
with Section 149(6) of the Companies Act, 2013 (“Act”). The maximum tenure of Independent Directors is compliant with the 
requirements of the Act. All the Independent Directors have confirmed that they meet the criteria of independence as mentioned 
under Regulation 16(1)(b) of the SEBI Listing Regulations read with Section 149(6) of the Act.
BOARD MEETINGS
The Company places before the Board all the pertinent and essential information at their meetings for the information and ap -
proval of the Board. During the financial year ended March 31, 2026, the Board met 7 (Seven) times on May 19, 2025, June 30, 
2025, August 05, 2025, November 12, 2025, November 24, 2025, February 13, 2026, and March 20, 2026.

BRIEF DESCRIPTION OF THE TERMS OF REFERENCE OF THE AUDIT COMMITTEE
The Audit Committee is responsible for the discharge of its statutory role as per framework provided under the Act and the 
applicable SEBI rules and regulations. It covers, inter alia, the following:
1. Oversight of the Company’s financial reporting process and the disclosure of its financial information to ensure that the 
financial statements are correct, sufficient and credible;
2. Recommendation for appointment, re-appointment, replacement, remuneration and terms of appointment of auditors 
(including chief internal auditor of the Company and external auditors) and the fixation of the audit fee;
3. Approval of payment to statutory auditors or external auditors for any other services rendered by them;
4. Reviewing, the financial statements with respect to its unlisted Subsidiary(ies), in particular investments made by such 
Subsidiary(ies);
5. Reviewing, with the management, the quarterly, half-yearly and annual financial statements and auditor’s report thereon 
before submission to the Board for approval;
=== page 104 ===
1036. Reviewing, with the management, the statement of uses / application of funds raised through an issue (public issue, rights 
issue, preferential issue, etc.), the statement of funds utilized for purposes other than those stated in the offer document / 
prospectus / notice and the report submitted by the monitoring agency monitoring the utilisation of proceeds of a public or 
rights issue, and making appropriate recommendations to the Board to take up steps in this matter;
7. Reviewing and monitoring the statutory auditor’s and Internal Auditors independence and performance and discuss the 
same with the management and review effectiveness and adequacy of audit process and review Internal audit reports 
relating to internal control weaknesses;
8. Approval of any subsequent modification of transactions of the Company with related parties and omnibus approval for 
related party transactions proposed to be entered into by the Company, subject to the conditions as may be prescribed and 
mandatorily review Statement of significant related party transactions;
9. Scrutiny of inter-corporate loans and investments;
10. Valuation of undertakings or assets of the Company, wherever it is necessary;
11. Evaluation of internal financial controls and risk management systems;
12. Reviewing the findings of any internal investigations by the internal auditors into matters where there is suspected fraud or 
irregularity or a failure of internal control systems of a material nature and reporting the matter to the Board;
13. Reviewing compliance with internal and statutory audit reports and examine reasons for substantial defaults and delays in 
implementing audit recommendations;
14. Discussion with statutory auditors before the audit commences, about the nature and scope of audit as well as post-audit 
discussion to ascertain any area of concern;
15. Looking into the reasons for substantial defaults in the payment to depositors, debenture holders, shareholders (in case of 
Non-payment of declared dividends) and creditors;
16. Reviewing the functioning of the whistle blower mechanism;
17. Review of statutory compliances and legal cases;
18. Approval of appointment of CFO after assessing the qualifications, experience and background, etc. of the candidate; and
19. Carrying out any other functions required to be carried out by the Audit Committee in terms of applicable law.
20. Mandatorily review Management discussion and analysis of financial condition and results of operations;
21. Management letter / letters of internal control weaknesses issued by the statutory auditors;
22. Statement of deviations in terms of the SEBI Listing Regulations:
a. Quarterly statement of deviation(s) including report of monitoring agency, if applicable, submitted to stock exchange(s) 
in terms of the SEBI Listing Regulations;
b. Annual statement of funds utilised for purposes other than those stated in the offer document/prospectus/notice in 
terms of the SEBI Listing Regulations.
23. Consider and comment on rationale, cost-benefits and impact of schemes involving merger, demerger, amalgamation etc., 
on the listed entity and its shareholders.

The Risk Management Committee shall be responsible for, among other things,  as may be required by the stock exchanges from

1. To formulate a detailed risk management policy in conformity with the requirements of the LODR regulations as amended 
from time to time. 
2. To ensure that appropriate methodology, processes and systems are in place to monitor and evaluate risks associated with 
the business of the Company. For instance, validating the process of Risk Management, the procedure for Risk Minimization, 
Cyber Security risk management and mitigation; 
3. To monitor and oversee implementation of the risk management policy, including evaluating the adequacy of risk 
management systems. This would include the activity of continually obtaining reasonable assurance from management that 
all known and emerging risks have been identified and mitigated or managed;
=== page 110 ===
1094. To periodically review the risk management policy, at least once in two years (or such other frequency stipulated by law from 
time to time), including by considering the changing industry dynamics and evolving complexity;
5. To keep the board of Directors informed about the nature and content of its discussions, recommendations and actions to be 
taken;
6. The appointment, removal and terms of remuneration of the Chief Risk Officer (if any) shall be subject to review by the Risk 
Management Committee
7. To coordinate its activities with other committees, in instances where there is an overlap with activities of such committees, 
as per the framework laid down by the board of Directors.
8. To seek information from any employee, obtain outside legal or other professional advice and secure attendance of outsiders 
with relevant expertise, if it considers necessary.
9. To carry out any other function as is mandated by the Board from time to time and / or enforced by any statutory notification, 
amendment or modification as may be applicable.
10. Develop and maintain a framework for identification and assessment of internal and external risks specifically faced by 
the listed entity, including but not limited to financial, operational, sectoral, sustainability (particularly, ESG related risks), 
information security, cyber security risks or any other risk as may be determined by the Committee
11. Measures for risk mitigation including systems and processes for internal control of identified risks
12. Ensure the development, implementation, and regular testing of a business continuity plan to address potential disruptions 
effectively.

17. FOREIGN EXCHANGE RISK AND HEDGING ACTIVITIES:
The risk of exchange rate volatility is mitigated by splitting and spreading the foreign exchange payments between the date of 
issue of import orders and the final payment against receipt of supplies. The Company has obtained the necessary authorisations 
for covering the foreign exchange exposure. The decision to avail hedging is taken on a case to case basis.

19. OTHER DISCLOSURES:
(a) Materially significant related party transactions:
 During the financial year under review, the Company acquired the remaining 30% equity stake in its subsidiary Swiss 
Parenterals Limited from Mr. Naishadh Shah, a related party, through a share swap arrangement. In consideration of the 
acquisition, the Company allotted 23,06,372 (Twenty Three Lakhs Six Thousand Three Hundred and Seventy Two) equity 
shares on a preferential basis to Mr. Naishadh Shah. Pursuant to the transaction, the subsidiary became a wholly owned 
subsidiary of the Company. The transaction was undertaken on an arm’s length basis and in compliance with the applicable 
provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015, 
after obtaining the requisite approvals.
 Other than the aforesaid transaction, there were no materially significant related party transactions during the year under 
review that may have potential conflict with the interests of the Company at large.
(b) Details of Non-compliance by the Company, penalties and strictures imposed on the Company by the Stock Exchanges or Securities 
and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) or any Authority on any matter related to capital markets during the last three years:  
 During the financial year under review, the Company received notices from BSE Limited and National Stock Exchange of India 
Limited imposing monetary penalties for non-compliance with the requirements relating to the composition of the Board of 
Directors under the applicable provisions of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015. 
The penalties imposed by each stock exchange amounted to `65,000/- (Rupees Sixty Five Thousand only) plus applicable 
GST vide their letters dated February 27, 2026 (Q4 2025), and `3,60,000/- (Rupees Three Lakh Sixty Thousand only) plus 
applicable GST vide their letters dated May 27, 2026 (Q1 2026). The non-compliance did not have any material impact on 
the financial, operational or other activities of the Company, except to the extent of the monetary penalties imposed.
=== page 117 ===
116 Other than the aforesaid, the Company had not received any notice regarding any non-compliance of the provisions of 
applicable laws to the Company which are material in nature.
(c) Whistle Blower Policy/Vigil Mechanism:
 In accordance with the requirements of the Act, read with SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations, 2015, the Company has in place the Whistle Blower Policy approved by the Board of Directors. The Company 
affirms that no personnel has been denied access to the Audit Committee. The said policy has been uploaded on the website 
of the Company i.e. https://www.eris.co.in/investor-relations/corporate-governance
(d) Compliance with mandatory requirements and adoption of Non-mandatory requirements:
 The Company has complied with all mandatory requirements of Regulation 34 of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure

Regulation 27 read with Part E of Schedule II of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations,

website of the Company i.e. https://www.eris.co.in/investor-relations/corporate-governance
(g) Commodity Price Risk and Hedging Activities:
 The Company purchases Active Pharmaceutical Ingredient (API) and other materials that are used in the manufacturing of 
drugs. The prices of raw materials generally fluctuate in line with commodity cycles over a short period of time.
 Commodity price risk exposure is evaluated and managed through operating procedures and sourcing policies. The Company 
also has a Risk Management framework to proactively mitigate the impact through measures like cost based price increases, 
cost reduction measures, portfolio rationalisation, renegotiating procurement contracts etc. Additionally, the Company also 
develops on an ongoing basis alternate supply sources for key products subject to economic justification.
 Most of these materials are sourced from the domestic market and therefore do not have significant foreign exchange 
fluctuation risks. The Company does not use any derivative contracts to hedge exposure to fluctuations in commodity prices.
(h) Details of the utilization of funds raised through preferential allotment or Qualified Institutions Placement as specified under 
Regulation 32 (7A):
 During the financial year under review, the Company has not raised any funds through preferential allotment or Qualified 
Institutions Placement.

Directors on the Board of the Company have been debarred or disqualified from being appointed or continuing as Directors 
of companies by the SEBI, Ministry of Corporate Affairs or any such other Statutory Authority, as stipulated under Regulation 
34(3) of the Listing Regulations. Copy of the said certificate appears at “Annexure A”  to this report. 
(j) There were no instances where the Board of Directors had not accepted any of the recommendation(s) given by any

134Annexure 9
POLICY ON REMUNERATION OF DIRECTORS, KEY MANAGERIAL PERSONNEL 
(“KMP”) AND OTHER EMPLOYEES
Preamble
This Policy on Remuneration of Directors, Key Managerial Personnel (“KMP”) and Other Employees (hereinafter referred as 
the “Policy”) of Eris Lifesciences Limited (“the Company”) is designed and formulated by the Nomination and Remuneration 
Committee (”the Committee”) of the Company pursuant to the Companies Act, 2013 (the “Act”) and rules made thereunder 
and the SEBI (Listing Obligations And Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015. In case of any inconsistency between 
the provisions of law and this remuneration policy, the provisions of the law shall prevail and the Company shall abide by the 
applicable law.
The Board of Directors of the Company adopted this policy at their meeting held on August 17, 2017.
The Policy lays down the criteria with regard to remuneration of Directors, KMP and other employees.
A. Guiding Principles for remuneration:  The Company shall remunerate all its personnel reasonably and sufficiently as per in -
dustry benchmarks and standards. The remuneration shall be commensurate with their contributions and shall be sufficient 
enough to retain and motivate the human resources of the Company. The compensation package will, inter alia, take into 
account the experience of the personnel, the knowledge and skill required including complexity of the job, work duration 
and risks associated with the work, and attitude of the worker like positive outlook, team work, loyalty, past remuneration, 
past performance etc.
 The level and components of the remuneration shall be such so as to align with the long term interest of the company and 
it’s shareholders.
B. Components of Remuneration :
 The following will be the various remuneration components which may be paid to the personnel of the Company based on 
the designation and class of the personnel.
a. Fixed compensation: The fixed salaries of the Company’s personnel shall be competitive and based on the individual 
personnel’s responsibilities and performance.
b. Variable compensation: The personnel of the Company may be paid remuneration by way of variable salaries based on 
their performance evaluation. Such variable salaries should be based on the performance of the individual against his 
short and long term performance objectives and 
 the performance of the Company.
c. Share based payments: The Board of Directors may, on the recommendation of the Nomination and Remuneration 
Committee, issue to certain class of personnel a share and share price related incentive program.
d. Non-monetary benefits: Senior management personnel of the Company may, on a case to case basis, be awarded 
customary non-monetary benefits such as discounted salary advance /credit facility, rent free accommodation, Company 
cars with or without chauffer’s, share and share price related incentive, reimbursement of electricity and telephone bills 
etc.
e. Commission: The directors may be paid commission if approved by the shareholders. The shareholders may authorise 
the Board to declare commission to be paid to any director of the Board.
f. Retirement benefits: The Company shall provide retirement benefits applicable in accordance with law.
g. Sitting Fee and Commission: The Company may pay sitting fee for attending Board and Committee meeting and 
commission to the Directors of the Company in compliance with law.
=== page 136 ===
135h. Loan/ advances to the Employees: The Company may give loan or advances to the employees in accordance with the 
provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and the terms and conditions of the Loan Policy of the Company, as approved by 
the Board or any Committees thereof, from time to time.
C. Entitlement : The authority to determine the entitlement to various components as aforesaid for each class and designation 
of personnel shall be as follows:
Designation / Class To be determined by
Managing Director/ Whole Time Director The remuneration for the Managing Director/ Whole Time 
Director is as per the agreement approved by the shareholders 
on recommendation of the Board of Directors. In case of 
any change, the same would require the approval of the 
shareholders on recommendation of the Board of Directors 
and other applicable compliances required by laws.
Independent Directors* Board of Directors / Executive Committee
Other Directors Board of Directors
Senior Management Recommendation of the Nomination and Remuneration 
Committee and approval of Board of Directors
Other Employee Departmental Heads in consultation with Human Resources 
Head
*Sitting fee payable to the directors shall be in accordance with the provisions of the law.
D. Amendment : The Remuneration policy may be reviewed by the Board of the Company on the recommendation of the 
Nomination & Remuneration Committee of the Board.
